[{"id":81574,"date":"2026-06-25T07:56:09","date_gmt":"2026-06-25T07:56:09","guid":{"rendered":"https:\/\/fintelegram.com\/?p=81574"},"modified":"2026-06-25T07:56:12","modified_gmt":"2026-06-25T07:56:12","slug":"mica-deadline-1-july-2026-esma-eu-crypto-grey-zone-poland","status":"publish","type":"post","link":"https:\/\/fintelegram.com\/mica-deadline-1-july-2026-esma-eu-crypto-grey-zone-poland\/","title":{"rendered":"MiCA Countdown: Europe\u2019s Crypto Grey Zone Dies On 1 July 2026 \u2014 And Poland May Become The First Regulatory Casualty"},"content":{"rendered":"\n
**FinTelegram Regulatory Intelligence Brief<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n
A regulatory earthquake is about to hit the European crypto market. On 1 July 2026, the last major transitional window under the EU\u2019s **[Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA)<\/a><\/strong> closes. The era of national VASP registrations, legal arbitrage, offshore servicing models, and \u201cwe are still applying\u201d excuses is over. From that date, crypto-asset service providers serving EU clients need a proper MiCA authorisation as a **Crypto-Asset Service Provider (CASP)<\/strong>. No authorisation, no business. No informal tolerance. No national grey zone. No magic \u201creverse solicitation\u201d shield for platforms that are actively hunting European customers.<\/p>\n\n\n\n This is not merely another compliance deadline. It is the regulatory line in the sand between the old European crypto market and the new one.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## The End Of The Grandfathering Illusion<\/h2>\n\n\n\n When MiCA\u2019s CASP regime became applicable on 30 December 2024, many crypto brokers, exchanges, custodians, and platforms treated the transitional regime as a comfortable waiting room. Under Article 143(3) MiCA, providers already operating lawfully under national regimes before 30 December 2024 could, in certain Member States, continue operating until 1 July 2026 or until they were granted or refused MiCA authorisation, whichever came first.<\/p>\n\n\n\n That waiting room is now closing.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ESMA has made the **[message brutally clear<\/a><\/strong>: after 1 July 2026, an unauthorised CASP providing crypto-asset services to EU clients is in breach of EU law and must stop. Providers that fail to obtain authorisation must not pretend that a pending application, old national VASP registration, group licence, offshore entity, or brand-level reputation is enough.<\/p>\n\n\n\n MiCA authorisation attaches to a specific legal entity. It is not a marketing badge. It is not transferable by logo. It is not inherited by affiliates. It is not a passport for the entire corporate group unless the authorised entity and the notified services actually match the client-facing business.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## The New Rule: Get Licensed Or Get Out<\/h2>\n\n\n\n From 1 July 2026, the compliance logic becomes brutally simple: **No MiCA CASP authorisation means no EU crypto services.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n Unauthorised providers must stop onboarding new EU clients, stop opening new client accounts, cease marketing and solicitation, and limit their activity to the orderly closing, transfer, or liquidation of existing positions. Custody can continue only for as long as strictly necessary to execute an orderly wind-down.<\/p>\n\n\n\n This is where the new enforcement phase begins. The question for regulators, banks, payment institutions, compliance officers, victims, and investigators is no longer whether a crypto platform once appeared on a local VASP register. The question is whether the exact legal entity providing the service is authorised under MiCA and properly passported for the relevant EU markets.<\/p>\n\n\n\n That changes everything.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## The Offshore Problem: Europe\u2019s Crypto Back Door Is Closing<\/h2>\n\n\n\n The biggest impact will not be on the cleanly authorised players. They have spent the last two years preparing. The impact will be on the platforms that built their business on regulatory fog.<\/p>\n\n\n\n Offshore exchanges and brokers using entities in loosely regulated jurisdictions will no longer be able to approach EU clients unless they fall within the very narrow reverse solicitation exception. And that exception is not a marketing strategy. It is a legal defence for genuinely client-initiated contact.<\/p>\n\n\n\n If a platform runs EU-facing ads, uses European languages, works through affiliates targeting EU traffic, pays introducers, runs German-, French-, Polish-, Italian- or Spanish-language funnels, or maintains EU customer acquisition dashboards, the \u201creverse solicitation\u201d argument becomes a fragile fiction.<\/p>\n\n\n\n In FinTelegram terms: the marketing trail becomes the evidence trail.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## The Travel Rule Trap<\/h2>\n\n\n\n MiCA is not arriving alone. The EU Transfer of Funds Regulation has already extended Travel Rule obligations to crypto-asset transfers. That means originator and beneficiary information, transaction monitoring, sanctions screening, suspicious activity reporting, and record-keeping are no longer optional compliance theatre.<\/p>\n\n\n\n For unauthorised or semi-authorised crypto platforms, this creates a second pressure point. Even during wind-down, AML\/CFT controls must remain effective. Shadow payment rails, opaque third-party processors, nominee merchant structures, and fragmented custody arrangements will become red flags, not operational conveniences.<\/p>\n\n\n\n In other words, after 1 July 2026, \u201ccrypto compliance\u201d in Europe is no longer only about the licence. It is about whether the entire transaction chain survives regulatory inspection.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Poland: MiCA On Paper, Regulatory Chaos In Practice<\/h2>\n\n\n\n Poland is emerging as the most dramatic case study in the final days before the MiCA deadline. The country did not simply ignore MiCA. In May 2026, Polish lawmakers adopted a crypto-assets bill designed to align the national framework with the EU\u2019s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation. But that legislative sprint collapsed into political deadlock when President Karol Nawrocki vetoed the bill on 11 June 2026 \u2014 reportedly for the third time.<\/p>\n\n\n\n That makes Poland\u2019s situation especially explosive. On paper, the country moved towards MiCA implementation. In practice, the veto left the domestic supervisory machinery unresolved just days before the EU transitional deadline. The result is a regulatory cliff-edge for Polish legacy VASPs: the EU deadline is fixed, but the national authorisation framework remains politically blocked.<\/p>\n\n\n\n The **Polish Financial Supervision Authority, KNF,<\/strong> has [already warned<\/a> that the 1 July 2026 deadline cannot be extended by national law or by a KNF decision. In the absence of a properly designated competent authority and effective national implementation, domestic entities relying on the old Polish VASP framework risk losing the ability to continue providing crypto-asset services after the deadline unless they obtain a valid MiCA authorisation.<\/p>\n\n\n\n That is not a technical delay. It is regulatory trauma in real time.<\/p>\n\n\n\n The irony is hard to miss: EU-authorised CASPs from other Member States may still be able to passport services into Poland, while domestic Polish legacy VASPs may be pushed into suspension, relocation, or forced wind-down. The intended EU single market may therefore arrive in Poland not as a smooth transition, but as a regulatory shock \u2014 with foreign licensed players potentially entering while local players are trapped in a politically manufactured licensing vacuum.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Red Flags For Investigators, Banks And Victims<\/h2>\n\n\n\n The post-1 July environment creates a new set of forensic indicators.<\/p>\n\n\n\n **1. Fake passporting claims.<\/strong> If a platform claims to be \u201cregistered in Poland,\u201d \u201cregistered in the Czech Republic,\u201d or \u201cregulated in Europe,\u201d check the ESMA MiCA register. A legacy VASP registration is not the same as MiCA authorisation. A registration in one Member State is not automatically an EU-wide licence.<\/p>\n\n\n\n **2. Wrong legal entity.<\/strong> Many brands operate through multiple entities. The authorised entity may not be the entity contracting with the customer, holding assets, receiving fiat deposits, or operating the exchange interface. Under MiCA, the exact legal entity matters.<\/p>\n\n\n\n **3. Reverse solicitation theatre.<\/strong> A platform cannot aggressively market to EU clients and then claim that every customer arrived by their own exclusive initiative. Affiliate campaigns, geo-targeted landing pages, local-language funnels, and sales scripts are evidence.<\/p>\n\n\n\n **4. Offshore custody chains.<\/strong> ESMA has warned that non-EU entities cannot be used to provide MiCA services to EU clients through the back door. Custody, in particular, cannot simply be outsourced to an unauthorised third-country affiliate.<\/p>\n\n\n\n **5. Shadow payment rails.<\/strong> Unclear merchant descriptors, third-party payment processors, layered bank accounts, and unusual fiat-to-crypto routing patterns should be treated as red flags. Where MiCA authorisation is missing, payment flows often reveal the true operating model.<\/p>\n\n\n\n **6. \u201cApplication pending\u201d excuses.<\/strong> A pending CASP application is not a licence. After 1 July 2026, the difference between authorised and unauthorised becomes existential.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## The Enforcement Phase Begins<\/h2>\n\n\n\n MiCA was often sold as Europe\u2019s attempt to bring legal certainty to crypto. That is true \u2014 but legal certainty cuts both ways. It protects authorised providers and clients. It also removes excuses for those who continue to operate outside the perimeter.<\/p>\n\n\n\n For years, Europe\u2019s crypto market lived with a patchwork of national registrations, loosely supervised VASP regimes, offshore group structures, and opportunistic passporting narratives. That structure now collapses into a binary test:<\/p>\n\n\n\n **Is the provider MiCA-authorised for the relevant service and client market \u2014 yes or no?<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n From 1 July 2026, that question becomes the first question banks, payment institutions, victims, investigators, regulators, and journalists should ask.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## FinTelegram Call For Information<\/h2>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram is asking insiders, compliance officers, former employees, payment processors, victims, and market participants to provide information on crypto exchanges, brokers, custodians, wallet operators, OTC desks, payment processors, and affiliate networks that continue to target EU clients after 1 July 2026 without valid MiCA authorisation.<\/p>\n\n\n\n We are particularly interested in:<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n* platforms claiming EU access through legacy VASP registrations;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * offshore entities servicing EU clients through EU-facing brands;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * payment processors facilitating unauthorised crypto services;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * misleading passporting claims;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * reverse-solicitation abuse;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * forced client migration, blocked withdrawals, or opaque wind-down procedures;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * Polish legacy VASPs attempting to continue operations after the deadline without authorisation.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\n Please submit documents, screenshots, payment records, customer communications, legal entity names, merchant descriptors, affiliate materials, and internal compliance information via Whistle42. The European crypto grey zone is closing. The next scandal will not be about who failed to apply for a licence. It will be about who kept taking European clients after the licence deadline had already passed.<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n [Share Information via Whistle42<\/a><\/div>\n<\/div>\n","protected":false},"excerpt":{"rendered":" The MiCA transitional period ends on 1 July 2026. Spinsopotamia\u2019s Vanishing Act \u2014 Zentoria\u2019s Front-Door Descriptor Now Appears Parked on GoDaddy"},"content":{"rendered":"\n After FinTelegram\u2019s reporting on **Zentoria, Spinsopotamia, NovaForge<\/strong> and the **NALMI<\/strong> casino-domain environment, the public-facing **Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> front has taken another curious turn. In an earlier update, FinTelegram reported that **Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong>, the front-door descriptor associated with **Zentoria Limited<\/strong>, had moved into a **[403-access-denied posture<\/a><\/strong> after the Zentoria \/ NALMI exposure. In other words: the door was still there, but visitors were no longer welcome.<\/p>\n\n\n\n Now the story appears to have moved one step further. A current screenshot reviewed by FinTelegram shows **spinsopotamia.com\/lander<\/strong> resolving to a **GoDaddy parking page<\/strong>, stating that \u201cspinsopotamia.com is parked free, courtesy of GoDaddy.com,\u201d with a prominent \u201cGet This Domain\u201d button.<\/p>\n\n\n\n That is quite a transformation for a domain that previously played a central role in FinTelegram\u2019s reporting as a payment-facing and public-facing anchor in the Zentoria casino-payment context.<\/p>\n\n\n\n Apparently, some casino front doors do not close. They quietly become real-estate listings.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Key Takeaways<\/h2>\n\n\n\n \n 1. **Spinsopotamia.com now appears to resolve to a GoDaddy parking page.<\/strong> This follows FinTelegram\u2019s earlier observation that the domain had moved into a 403-access-denied posture.<\/li>\n\n\n\n * **The timing raises compliance questions.<\/strong> FinTelegram does not claim causation, but the sequence is notable in light of the Zentoria \/ NALMI reporting.<\/li>\n\n\n\n * **The key issue is merchant monitoring.<\/strong> If Spinsopotamia was used as a payment descriptor or merchant-facing domain, PSPs and acquirers should review whether payments continued after the domain became inaccessible or parked.<\/li>\n\n\n\n * **Domain parking does not prove wrongdoing.<\/strong> It does, however, challenge the presentation of Spinsopotamia as a stable, active, verifiable merchant front.<\/li>\n\n\n\n * **Provider-level records remain decisive.<\/strong> PSP, acquirer, registrar, DNS, infrastructure and payment records are needed to determine who controlled the domain and whether replacement domains or descriptors were used.<\/li>\n<\/ol>\n\n\n\n **[Read our Spinsopotamia 403 report here.<\/a><\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Why This Matters<\/h2>\n\n\n \n![Image 1: \"The](https://fintelegram.com/%22data:image//svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB3aWR0aD0iMSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxIiB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciPjwvc3ZnPg==/%22)<\/figure>\n<\/div>\n\n\n **Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> was not just any random domain in FinTelegram\u2019s previous reporting. It was the domain associated with the public-facing **Spinsopotamia<\/strong> front and the payment-descriptor questions surrounding **Zentoria Limited<\/strong>, an Irish-registered gambling-sector entity.<\/p>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram\u2019s prior reporting raised the question whether Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia operated as a clean EU-facing merchant or descriptor layer connected to offshore casino brands and payment flows.<\/p>\n\n\n\n Separately, an external technical evidence package prepared for FinTelegram placed the Spinsopotamia anchor inside a much broader casino-domain infrastructure environment associated with **NALMI \/ AS213846<\/strong>, while also documenting configuration, API, telemetry and application-layer correlations across a large number of casino-related domains.<\/p>\n\n\n\n The new GoDaddy parking page does not prove why the domain changed status. But it does raise fresh questions:<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n * Has Zentoria abandoned Spinsopotamia as a public-facing domain?<\/li>\n\n\n\n * Is the Spinsopotamia descriptor still used in payment flows?<\/li>\n\n\n\n * If yes, why would a payment descriptor point to a parked domain?<\/li>\n\n\n\n * Has the operation moved to another front-door domain?<\/li>\n\n\n\n * Did PSPs, acquirers or regulators monitor the change?<\/li>\n\n\n\n * Who currently controls the domain and its DNS settings?<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\n These are not cosmetic questions. In high-risk gambling payments, a merchant descriptor should point to a clear, active and verifiable merchant presence \u2014 not to a registrar parking page.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## From Front Door to 403 to Parking Page<\/h2>\n\n\n\n The observed sequence is notable:<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n 1. **Spinsopotamia.com was identified in FinTelegram reporting as a Zentoria-facing public and payment-related anchor.<\/strong><\/li>\n\n\n\n * **After the Zentoria \/ NALMI exposure, the domain appeared to move into a 403-access-denied posture.<\/strong><\/li>\n\n\n\n * **It now appears on a GoDaddy parking \/ lander page.<\/strong><\/li>\n<\/ol>\n\n\n\n This sequence does not prove causation. It does, however, look like a classic post-exposure retreat pattern: restrict access, remove public-facing functionality, then let the domain fall back into registrar parking or sales presentation.<\/p>\n\n\n\n In less technical language: the front door first stopped opening, and now the sign outside says the shop may be available.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Compliance Questions for PSPs and Acquirers<\/h2>\n\n\n\n For banks, PSPs, card acquirers and wallet providers, the critical question is straightforward:<\/p>\n\n\n\n **Was \u201cSpinsopotamia.com\u201d ever used \u2014 or is it still being used \u2014 as a merchant descriptor, payment reference, cashier label or onboarding website?<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n If the answer is yes, then the domain\u2019s apparent parked status becomes highly relevant for merchant monitoring. A payment institution cannot reasonably treat a descriptor as low-risk if the corresponding public-facing website is no longer an operating merchant site.<\/p>\n\n\n\n At a minimum, any PSP, acquirer or bank previously exposed to Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia should review:<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n * merchant onboarding files;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * approved descriptors;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * website verification records;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * current domain and DNS status;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * transaction-monitoring alerts;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * linked casino brands;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * MIDs, gateway IDs and settlement beneficiaries;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * and whether payments continued after the domain became inaccessible or parked.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\n ## What This Does Not Prove<\/h2>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram does not claim that Zentoria shut down Spinsopotamia because of FinTelegram\u2019s reporting.<\/p>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram does not claim that the GoDaddy parking page proves illegal conduct.<\/p>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram does not claim that every domain in the broader NALMI environment is owned or controlled by Zentoria.<\/p>\n\n\n\n The observation is narrower but important: **a domain previously relevant to FinTelegram\u2019s Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia reporting now appears to be parked rather than operating as a live merchant or casino front.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n That is a compliance event worth recording.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## FinTelegram Assessment<\/h2>\n\n\n\n The apparent parking of Spinsopotamia.com strengthens one of the core questions in the Zentoria case: was Spinsopotamia a stable, legitimate public-facing gambling site \u2014 or was it a disposable front-door layer in a broader offshore casino-payment structure?<\/p>\n\n\n\n The answer requires provider-level records. But the pattern is increasingly difficult to ignore. First came the descriptor questions. Then came the NALMI infrastructure correlations. Then came the 403. Now comes the GoDaddy parking page. For a supposedly clean front door, Spinsopotamia has become remarkably difficult to enter.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Right of Reply<\/h2>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram invites **Zentoria Limited, NALMI Limited<\/strong>, relevant payment processors, acquirers, wallet providers, infrastructure providers, domain registrars, casino operators and any other affected party to provide clarification, corrections or documentary evidence. Substantiated responses will be reviewed and reflected in updated reporting where appropriate.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Call for Information<\/h2>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram invites insiders, payment professionals, former employees, compliance officers, affected players, affiliates and infrastructure specialists to provide information about Zentoria, Spinsopotamia, NovaForge, NALMI and related casino-payment flows.<\/p>\n\n\n\n We are especially interested in:<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n * bank or card statements showing Spinsopotamia or Zentoria descriptors;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * cashier screenshots from related casino brands;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * payment confirmation emails;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * merchant receipts;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * PSP or acquirer onboarding records;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * domain-control or DNS records;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * information about replacement domains or new descriptors;<\/li>\n\n\n\n * records identifying settlement beneficiaries behind the relevant casino deposits.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\n Information can be shared confidentially with FinTelegram via Whistle42.<\/p>\n\n\n\n \n [Share Information via Whistle42<\/a><\/div>\n<\/div>\n","protected":false},"excerpt":{"rendered":" After FinTelegram\u2019s reporting on Zentoria, Spinsopotamia and the NALMI casino-domain environment, the Spinsopotamia.com front appears to have moved from a 403-access-denied posture to a GoDaddy parking page. casino anchor has, for the moment, slammed shut. As of **23 June 2026 (08:00 UTC)<\/strong>, requests to **spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> no longer return a working website. Instead, the server answers every request with the same terse verdict: **HTTP 403 \u2014 “Access denied.”<\/strong> That is a small string of text with a large amount of context behind it. <\/p>\n\n\n\n FinTelegram can now document a **sudden, global shutdown<\/strong> of the **Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> front descriptor that sits at the heart of the **Zentoria \/ NALMI<\/strong> casino ecosystem. In the immediate wake of our **[Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia and the NALMI Casino Network<\/a><\/strong> report and the new Technical Annex, the previously live site now responds with **HTTP 403 Forbidden<\/strong> to every tested request \u2013 yet the infrastructure behind it remains fully reachable and responsive.<\/p>\n\n\n\n This is not a dead domain quietly expiring in a corner of the internet. The hostname still resolves to **185.207.197.216<\/strong>, Cloudflare still fronts the service, TLS on port 443 still completes cleanly, and the server still returns a valid HTML body: a blunt **\u201cAccess denied\u201d<\/strong>. Spinsopotamia did not disappear; someone flipped it into an active denial state.<\/p>\n\n\n\n ## Panic mode: from live front end to global 403<\/h2>\n\n\n\n The new technical record shows exactly what changed:<\/p>\n\n\n \n![Image 2: \"\"](https://fintelegram.com/%22data:image//svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB3aWR0aD0iMSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxIiB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciPjwvc3ZnPg==/%22)<\/figure>\n<\/div>\n\n\n \n * Until shortly before our reporting, **Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> was serving a full front end and operating normally.<\/li>\n\n\n\n * Immediately **after<\/strong> our report set went live, five independent request variants \u2013 browser GET to `\/<\/code>, browser GET to \/en\/<\/code>, default curl GET, HEAD request, and direct-IP GET against 185.207.197.216<\/strong> with Host: spinsopotamia.com \u2013 all returned HTTP 403 Forbidden<\/strong>.<\/li>\n\n\n\nEach response carried a Server: cloudflare<\/code> header, unique CF-RAY<\/strong> identifiers with PRG location codes, and a __cf_bm<\/code> cookie, while four variants delivered an identical 13-byte body<\/strong>: \u201cAccess denied\u201d<\/strong> (SHA-256 hash matching across the set).<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nThe browser view is equally stark. Compliance Report: Zentoria, Spinsopotamia and NALMI \u2014 A Plain-English Map of a Casino Payment and Infrastructure Cluster"},"content":{"rendered":"\nHow a Zentoria-facing payment fa\u00e7ade, a concentrated NALMI infrastructure environment and a multi-brand casino platform layer may fit together \u2014 and what regulators, banks and PSPs should ask next.<\/strong><\/h4>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n2-Minutes Briefing<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThis Explainer Compliance Report translates the findings in the recently published compliance report<\/a> into a plain-English network model. It explains Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia as the EU-facing front-door layer, NALMI as the infrastructure envelope<\/strong>, and the wider casino-domain ecosystem as a multi-brand platform environment with recurring configuration, API, telemetry and application-build similarities. The report identifies key disclosure targets for regulators, payment providers and banks.<\/p>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram has been tracking how Zentoria Limited<\/strong>, an Irish-registered gambling-sector entity, and its public-facing domain Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> appear in connection with offshore casino brands, player deposits and payment descriptors. Earlier reporting focused primarily on the payments side: player statements, casino cashier flows, possible merchant descriptors and the question whether a clean EU-facing entity was being used as a payment fa\u00e7ade for higher-risk offshore gambling brands.<\/p>\n\n\n\nA new technical evidence package prepared by an external network engineer working with FinTelegram adds a different layer: infrastructure and platform correlation.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe core finding is not that Zentoria has been proven to own a large casino network. Nor is it that NALMI has been proven to operate illegal casinos. The stronger and more defensible point is narrower but highly significant:<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe Zentoria-facing Spinsopotamia.com anchor is technically located inside the same concentrated NALMI \/ AS213846 infrastructure environment as hundreds of casino-related domains, many of which also show repeated configuration, API, telemetry and application-build similarities.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nIn plain English: Spinsopotamia does not look like an isolated casino website. It appears to sit inside or alongside a broader technical environment used by a large population of casino domains. That matters for regulators, banks, acquirers, wallet providers and payment institutions because it raises a simple due-diligence question:<\/p>\n\n\n\nWhen a payment provider onboards or services a merchant such as Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia, is it reviewing only the visible front door \u2014 or the wider casino infrastructure and operational ecosystem behind it?<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nThis report explains the model in non-technical terms.<\/p>\n\n\n\nGo to the Zentoria \/ NALMI Compliance Report! <\/a><\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\nKey Takeaways<\/h2>\n\n\n\n\nZentoria \/ Spinsopotamia appears as the EU-facing front-door layer.<\/strong> FinTelegram\u2019s prior reporting links Zentoria and Spinsopotamia to payment-descriptor and offshore-casino questions. The new report explains why that front-door role matters for PSPs, acquirers and banks.<\/li>\n\n\n\nNALMI appears as the infrastructure envelope.<\/strong> The technical evidence places almost the entire investigated casino-domain population inside the same NALMI \/ AS213846 network environment. This does not prove ownership, but it creates a highly relevant disclosure target.<\/li>\n\n\n\nThe wider casino ecosystem shows platform-like patterns.<\/strong> Repeated configuration markers, API dependencies, telemetry signals and byte-identical application assets suggest a shared or templated technical environment behind multiple casino brands.<\/li>\n\n\n\nThe evidence challenges the \u201cisolated website\u201d explanation.<\/strong> The strongest defensible conclusion is not that Zentoria owns the wider network, but that Spinsopotamia does not appear technically isolated from the broader casino-domain environment.<\/li>\n\n\n\nProvider-level records are now decisive.<\/strong> Infrastructure, edge, platform, telemetry, support and payment providers may hold the records needed to determine account ownership, tenant control, payment routing and settlement beneficiaries.<\/li>\n<\/ol>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n1. The Compliance Question<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe Zentoria \/ NALMI case should not be understood as a purely technical matter. It is a compliance architecture problem<\/strong>.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIn offshore gambling, the public-facing casino brand, the legal operator, the payment descriptor, the cashier provider, the platform operator and the hosting infrastructure are often separated across different jurisdictions and different entities. This fragmentation can make regulatory responsibility difficult to identify.<\/p>\n\n\n\nA player may believe they are depositing into one casino brand. Their bank statement may show another name. The website may display a licensed or legitimate-looking front. The payment may be processed through yet another provider. The technical infrastructure may support dozens or hundreds of related casino domains.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is the risk model FinTelegram is investigating. The key question is not only \u201cWho owns the casino?\u201d but also:<\/p>\n\n\n\nWho provides the front door, who provides the building, who provides the service corridors, and who moves the money?<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n2. Who Is Zentoria?<\/h2>\n\n\n\nIn FinTelegram\u2019s reporting, Zentoria Limited<\/strong> has emerged as a key EU-facing entity in the NovaForge \/ offshore casino context. Zentoria has been associated with Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong>, a domain that has reportedly appeared as a descriptor or public-facing anchor in connection with player deposits into offshore casino brands.<\/p>\n\n\n\nFor compliance purposes, Zentoria matters because it represents the layer that can appear legitimate to banks, acquirers and payment processors:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nan EU-registered company;<\/li>\n\n\n\na gambling-sector activity profile;<\/li>\n\n\n\na public-facing domain;<\/li>\n\n\n\na descriptor that may appear on payment statements;<\/li>\n\n\n\nand a cleaner presentation than the offshore casino brands receiving the player traffic.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nThis does not by itself prove wrongdoing. A licensed or registered gambling-related entity can operate lawfully. However, where such an entity appears in connection with deposits into offshore or blacklisted casino brands, the compliance question becomes more serious.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIn this model, Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia can be understood as the front-door layer<\/strong>. It is the visible name, the payment-facing wrapper and the apparently cleaner interface through which a higher-risk gambling ecosystem may access regulated financial rails.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n3. Who Is NALMI?<\/h2>\n\n\n\nNALMI Limited \/ AS213846<\/strong> appears in the technical evidence as the infrastructure environment where the overwhelming majority of the investigated casino-related domains are concentrated. The external technical report reviewed 496 casino-related domains. According to the evidence package, 495 of those domains were located inside the same routed network range:<\/p>\n\n\n\n185.207.196.0\/22 \u2014 AS213846 \/ NALMI Limited<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nSpinsopotamia.com was also observed within this same wider \/22 environment.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis does not mean NALMI is necessarily the owner or operator of all those casino domains. Hosting and routing providers can serve multiple customers. Infrastructure concentration is not the same as legal ownership.<\/p>\n\n\n\nBut from a compliance perspective, NALMI is highly relevant because it appears to provide or route the technical building<\/strong> in which the casino-domain population is concentrated.<\/p>\n\n\n\nA simple analogy helps:<\/p>\n\n\n\nNALMI is not necessarily the shop owner. It may be the building, the address system or the technical landlord. But hundreds of casino-related doors appear to be located in that same building.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is why regulators and payment providers should care.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n4. The Network in Plain English<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe technical evidence can be simplified into a four-layer model.<\/p>\n\n\n\nLayer 1 \u2014 The Front Door: Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThis is the layer visible to users, banks or payment institutions. It may appear as a website, descriptor, merchant reference or legal entity.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIn this model, Spinsopotamia.com<\/strong> is the public-facing anchor and Zentoria Limited<\/strong> is the entity associated with that anchor in FinTelegram\u2019s reporting.<\/p>\n\n\n\nLayer 2 \u2014 The Casino Brand Layer<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThis is the layer of outward-facing casino brands: names, skins, replacement domains, mirror domains and campaign domains. These brands may look separate to players. They may use different names, designs or landing pages.<\/p>\n\n\n\nBut technical evidence suggests that many such domains may not be truly independent at the infrastructure or platform level.<\/p>\n\n\n\nLayer 3 \u2014 The Service Corridor<\/h3>\n\n\n\nBehind the public casino brands, there appears to be a shared or templated service environment. This is where the technical evidence becomes important. The reports identify repeated elements such as:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nshared configuration markers;<\/li>\n\n\n\nrepeated fraud and telemetry endpoints;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncross-domain API dependencies;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncanonical and alternate API hosts;<\/li>\n\n\n\nrecurring support and analytics services;<\/li>\n\n\n\nand byte-identical application assets.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nIn non-technical language, this is the service corridor behind the casino doors<\/strong>. Different casino brands may appear separate from the outside, but behind the scenes they may be using common pipes, common back-office tools, common technical configurations or a common platform provider.<\/p>\n\n\n\nLayer 4 \u2014 The Infrastructure Building: NALMI<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThis is the network layer. The evidence places almost the entire investigated domain population inside the same NALMI \/ AS213846 network envelope.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis does not answer the ownership question, but it gives regulators a compact target area for disclosure requests.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n5. What the Technical Evidence Adds<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe technical evidence supplied to FinTelegram is important because it does not rely on branding impressions, website similarity or speculation. It relies on public-source technical artefacts.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe most important findings are:<\/p>\n\n\n\n5.1 The NALMI Concentration<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThe reviewed dataset contains 496 investigated casino-related domains. According to the evidence package, 495 were located inside the same NALMI \/ AS213846 network range.<\/p>\n\n\n\nSpinsopotamia.com, the Zentoria-facing anchor, was also observed inside that same routed environment.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis creates a strong infrastructure bridge between the Zentoria-facing front door and the wider casino-domain population.<\/p>\n\n\n\n5.2 The 83-Domain Configuration Cluster<\/h3>\n\n\n\nA narrower cluster of 83 domains showed repeated configuration markers across preserved public pages. These included the same CSP reporting endpoint, the same SEON fraud\/device-intelligence token, a repeated non-financial promotion-response hash and the same NALMI network envelope.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis is materially stronger than simply saying that many sites use the same cloud provider. These are account- or project-style markers that can point to a shared configuration environment.<\/p>\n\n\n\n5.3 The API and Mirror-Domain Pattern<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThe evidence also identifies a structure in which public-facing domains call other canonical or alternate API hosts. This pattern is consistent with rotating fa\u00e7ade domains and central backend services.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIn plain English: the visible casino domain may not be the real operational centre. It may be a shop window that connects to another technical host behind the scenes.<\/p>\n\n\n\n5.4 Byte-Identical Application Assets<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThe consolidated report identifies 120 meaningful byte-identical asset groups connecting 98 domains into 16 technical components.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis means that many outwardly separate casino domains delivered identical pieces of first-party application code. That is a classic sign of a shared deployment pipeline, shared platform template or centrally managed casino software environment.<\/p>\n\n\n\n5.5 Telemetry, Support and Service Reuse<\/h3>\n\n\n\nThe reports also identify repeated use of analytics, fraud-intelligence, session-replay, support and platform-related services across the domain population.<\/p>\n\n\n\nAgain, the significance is not that every provider acted unlawfully. The significance is that these providers may hold the account-level records that can resolve the attribution question.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n6. What Role Does Zentoria Appear to Play?<\/h2>\n\n\n\nBased on the evidence currently available, the safest formulation is:<\/p>\n\n\n\nZentoria appears to be the EU-facing public and payment-facing anchor in a broader casino-payment and infrastructure environment.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nThat does not mean Zentoria has been proven to own all related casino brands. It does not mean Zentoria controls the NALMI network. It does not mean every brand in the wider cluster is legally operated by Zentoria.<\/p>\n\n\n\nBut it does mean that Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia is not merely a random name in the story. It appears at the front end of the compliance problem: the point where a player, a bank, a payment institution or an acquirer may see a cleaner descriptor or a more conventional gambling-sector entity.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIn the network model, Zentoria is best described as:<\/p>\n\n\n\nthe clean front-door layer.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is a critical role. In high-risk payment ecosystems, the front-door layer can determine whether a transaction is accepted, how it is described, how it is monitored and whether a bank or acquirer understands the true risk profile of the underlying merchant activity.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n7. What Role Does NALMI Appear to Play?<\/h2>\n\n\n\nNALMI appears to be the infrastructure envelope<\/strong>.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe evidence places hundreds of casino-related domains inside the NALMI \/ AS213846 185.207.196.0\/22 network environment. This makes NALMI a key infrastructure disclosure target.<\/p>\n\n\n\nBut it is important not to overstate the point. The evidence does not prove that NALMI is the casino operator. It does not prove that NALMI owns the casino brands. It does not prove that NALMI designed the payment structure.<\/p>\n\n\n\nWhat it does show is that the casino-domain population is highly concentrated in a technical environment associated with NALMI. That gives regulators and compliance teams a focused place to ask questions:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nWho controls the relevant server allocations?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWhich customer accounts were assigned to the domains?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWere the domains provisioned for one customer or many?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWere abuse complaints received?<\/li>\n\n\n\nDid the same customer account control both the larger casino-domain population and the Spinsopotamia endpoint?<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nIn the network model, NALMI is best described as:<\/p>\n\n\n\nthe technical building or infrastructure layer.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n8. The Missing Middle: Platform and Operations<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe most important unresolved layer is the middle layer between Zentoria and NALMI.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis is the platform and operations layer. It may include:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\ncasino software providers;<\/li>\n\n\n\nAPI host operators;<\/li>\n\n\n\nsupport and CRM tenants;<\/li>\n\n\n\nanalytics and telemetry accounts;<\/li>\n\n\n\nfraud-screening accounts;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncashier and wallet providers;<\/li>\n\n\n\npayment gateways and acquirers;<\/li>\n\n\n\naffiliate and campaign operators;<\/li>\n\n\n\ndomain managers;<\/li>\n\n\n\nand whoever controls deployment and configuration.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nThis is where legal attribution will likely be resolved.<\/p>\n\n\n\nPublic-source evidence can show that many doors lead into the same building and that many shops use similar tools. It cannot always identify who signed the lease, who paid the invoices, who controlled the admin panel or who received the settlement funds.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is why provider-side disclosure is essential.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n9. Why This Matters for Regulators and Payment Providers<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe Zentoria \/ NALMI case illustrates a broader problem in online gambling compliance.<\/p>\n\n\n\nTraditional merchant due diligence often looks at the visible merchant:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nthe company name;<\/li>\n\n\n\nthe registered address;<\/li>\n\n\n\nthe licence or registration;<\/li>\n\n\n\nthe website shown at onboarding;<\/li>\n\n\n\nand the descriptor shown to card networks or banks.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nBut modern offshore casino ecosystems may be layered. A clean merchant descriptor can sit in front of offshore brands. A licensed or registered entity can be used as a payment-facing wrapper. A large number of casino domains can rotate through shared infrastructure. Payment flows can be separated from brand ownership. Platform providers can centralise the real operating environment.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis means that onboarding a merchant such as Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia cannot stop at checking one website.<\/p>\n\n\n\nA serious compliance review should ask:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nWhich casino brands are actually funded through this merchant?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWhich descriptors appear on player statements?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWhich domains share the same cashier, support or platform environment?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWhich infrastructure provider hosts or routes the related domains?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWhich payment gateways, wallet providers, MIDs and acquirers are used?<\/li>\n\n\n\nWho is the ultimate settlement beneficiary?<\/li>\n\n\n\nAre players in restricted jurisdictions being accepted?<\/li>\n\n\n\nAre offshore casino deposits being presented under a cleaner EU-facing descriptor?<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nThese are not technical questions. They are standard AML, merchant-risk and gambling-compliance questions.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n10. What This Report Does Not Say<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThis report does not claim that Zentoria owns the NALMI casino network.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt does not claim that NALMI operates illegal casinos.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt does not claim that every domain in the wider infrastructure population has the same legal owner or ultimate beneficial owner.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt does not claim that every provider detected in the technical evidence knowingly facilitated unlawful activity.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt does not establish criminal liability, regulatory breach or civil liability as a matter of law.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe evidence is technical and operational. It shows infrastructure concentration, configuration reuse, API relationships, application-build similarities and provider footprints. Final legal attribution requires provider records, payment records, corporate records and assessment by competent regulators.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat boundary matters. It is also what makes the evidence stronger.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n11. The Strongest Defensible Conclusion<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe strongest defensible conclusion is this:<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe technical evidence does not prove that Zentoria owns the NALMI casino network. But it makes one proposition increasingly difficult to sustain: that Spinsopotamia is merely an isolated, standalone casino site with no meaningful connection to the wider offshore casino infrastructure around it.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nThe evidence instead points to a layered model:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nZentoria \/ Spinsopotamia<\/strong> as the EU-facing and payment-facing front door;<\/li>\n\n\n\nmultiple casino brands and mirror domains<\/strong> as the visible brand layer;<\/li>\n\n\n\nshared APIs, telemetry, support, fraud and application assets<\/strong> as the service corridor;<\/li>\n\n\n\nand NALMI \/ AS213846<\/strong> as the concentrated infrastructure building.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nFor regulators, banks and payment institutions, the message is simple:<\/p>\n\n\n\nDo not review the front door alone. Map the building, the corridors and the rails behind it.<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n12. Disclosure Targets<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe next stage is not more speculation. It is disclosure.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe following parties or provider categories may hold the records needed to move from technical correlation to legal attribution:<\/p>\n\n\n\nDisclosure Target<\/th>Records That Matter<\/th><\/tr><\/thead>Infrastructure provider \/ NALMI<\/td>Customer accounts, server allocations, billing contacts, abuse records, routing history<\/td><\/tr>Edge \/ CDN provider<\/td>Zone ownership, DNS history, origin configuration, WAF and abuse records<\/td><\/tr>Platform provider<\/td>Tenant ownership, brand IDs, deployment history, authorised users<\/td><\/tr>Telemetry providers<\/td>Project ownership, masking settings, retention policies, authorised users<\/td><\/tr>Support \/ CRM providers<\/td>Tenant ownership, support portals, ticket routing, sender verification<\/td><\/tr>Payment \/ cashier providers<\/td>MIDs, gateway IDs, route IDs, acquirers, settlement beneficiaries<\/td><\/tr>Banks and card acquirers<\/td>Merchant onboarding files, transaction monitoring records, descriptor approvals<\/td><\/tr>Gambling regulators<\/td>Licence scope, operator responsibility, restricted-jurisdiction exposure<\/td><\/tr><\/tbody><\/table><\/figure>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n13. Call for Information<\/h2>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram invites whistleblowers, former employees, payment insiders, compliance officers, infrastructure specialists, affiliate managers, casino platform contractors and affected players to provide information about the Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia \/ NALMI environment.<\/p>\n\n\n\nWe are especially interested in:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nbank or card statements showing Spinsopotamia, Zentoria or related descriptors;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncashier screenshots from Robycasino, Spinsy, Kingmaker, BillyBets, MyEmpire, DuoSpin or related brands;<\/li>\n\n\n\nmerchant receipts, checkout pages, gateway IDs or payment confirmation emails;<\/li>\n\n\n\ninternal onboarding or compliance files involving Zentoria, Spinsopotamia, NovaForge or related entities;<\/li>\n\n\n\nprovider records linking domains, tenants, support portals or telemetry accounts;<\/li>\n\n\n\ninformation about the NALMI-hosted casino-domain environment;<\/li>\n\n\n\nrecords identifying the settlement beneficiaries behind the relevant payment flows.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nInformation can be shared confidentially with FinTelegram via Whistle42.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n14. Right of Reply<\/h2>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram invites Zentoria Limited, NALMI Limited, relevant platform providers, payment processors, acquirers, wallet providers, support providers, telemetry providers and any named or indirectly referenced party to provide clarification, corrections or documentary evidence.<\/p>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram will review substantiated responses and update its reporting where appropriate.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis report is published in the public interest as a compliance intelligence analysis. It is based on public-source technical evidence, prior FinTelegram reporting and whistleblower-supported research. No finding of unlawful conduct is made against any person, provider or company without further provider-level and regulatory confirmation.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nShare Information via Whistle42<\/a><\/div>\n<\/div>\n","protected":false},"excerpt":{"rendered":"FinTelegram explains the Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia \/ NALMI case in plain English: how an EU-facing payment and website anchor may sit inside a much wider offshore casino-domain infrastructure. ntelegram.com\/wp-json\/wp\/v2\/taxonomies\/post_tag"}],"wp:post_type":[{"href":"https:\/\/fintelegram.com\/wp-json\/wp\/v2\/posts?tags=16173"}],"curies":[{"name":"wp","href":"https:\/\/api.w.org\/{rel}","templated":true}]}}]]}},{"id":81551,"date":"2026-06-22T14:40:08","date_gmt":"2026-06-22T14:40:08","guid":{"rendered":"https:\/\/fintelegram.com\/?p=81551"},"modified":"2026-06-22T15:26:21","modified_gmt":"2026-06-22T15:26:21","slug":"softswiss-dream-finance-compliance-report-v1","status":"publish","type":"post","link":"https:\/\/fintelegram.com\/softswiss-dream-finance-compliance-report-v1\/","title":{"rendered":"FinTelegram Releases SoftSwiss \/ Dream Finance Compliance Report v1.0: iGaming Rails, Crypto Payments, MiCA Pressure and Ownership Questions"},"content":{"rendered":"\nFinTelegram has released the first version of its SoftSwiss \/ Dream Finance Compliance Intelligence Report<\/strong>, a structured risk-based dossier on the multi-jurisdiction iGaming and crypto-payment ecosystem surrounding SoftSwiss<\/strong>, Dream Finance<\/strong>, CoinsPaid<\/strong>, CryptoProcessing<\/strong>, FinteqHub<\/strong>, and related payment and casino infrastructure.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe report is now available for download.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis Compliance Report consolidates FinTelegram\u2019s fragmented reporting<\/strong> and reviewed source material into one structured intelligence dossier designed for regulators, financial institutions, payment service providers, acquirers, compliance teams, investigators, and affected market participants.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe purpose of the report is not to assert the legal liability or guilt of any named entity or individual. Rather, it describes a risk-relevant fact pattern<\/strong> and identifies where the available evidence supports documented findings, where it supports only inferences, and where allegations remain contested or unresolved.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nDownload: [SoftSwiss \/ Dream Finance Compliance Report v1.0 \u2013 PDF]<\/strong><\/a><\/div>\n<\/div>\n\n\n\nWhy This Report is Important<\/h2>\n\n\n\n<\/a><\/figure>\n<\/div>\n\n\nSoftSwiss is a major iGaming software and casino-platform provider<\/strong>. Dream Finance O\u00dc<\/strong>, through the CoinsPaid<\/strong> and CryptoProcessing<\/strong> brands, forms a material crypto-payment processing layer within the broader ecosystem. FinteqHub<\/strong>, publicly described by SoftSwiss as a payment gateway built by its payments team, adds a further payment-orchestration layer.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe ecosystem sits at the intersection of several high-risk domains:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nonline casino and iGaming infrastructure;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncrypto-payment processing;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncustomer-asset handling;<\/li>\n\n\n\npayment orchestration and merchant-of-record complexity;<\/li>\n\n\n\noffshore casino jurisdictions, including Cura\u00e7ao;<\/li>\n\n\n\nMiCA-driven regulatory transition;<\/li>\n\n\n\nAML\/CFT and sanctions-risk exposure;<\/li>\n\n\n\ncontested ownership and beneficial-control questions;<\/li>\n\n\n\nlitigation, whistleblower allegations, and reputational pressure.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nThis is precisely the type of cross-border cyberfinance and payment infrastructure case that requires structured compliance analysis rather than isolated article-by-article treatment.<\/p>\n\n\n\nEvidence Discipline: Facts, Allegations and Hypotheses<\/h2>\n\n\n\nA central feature of the report is its evidence-tier methodology. Each material statement is marked or assessed by evidentiary strength:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\n[DOC] Documented:<\/strong> registers, audited accounts, licence records, court documents, legal notices, or company disclosures.<\/li>\n\n\n\n[SELF] Public self-positioning:<\/strong> statements by the entities themselves through websites, marketing, legal hubs, or press materials.<\/li>\n\n\n\n[3P] Third-party reporting:<\/strong> external media, adverse-media sources, and public investigative reporting.<\/li>\n\n\n\n[WB] Whistleblower material:<\/strong> insider claims, named or anonymous, reviewed by FinTelegram.<\/li>\n\n\n\n[HYP] FinTelegram hypothesis:<\/strong> analytical inference or interpretation based on the evidence corpus.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nThe governing principle is simple: evidence is never upgraded by narrative<\/strong>. An allegation remains an allegation. A hypothesis remains a hypothesis. A correlation remains a correlation. Registered ownership and alleged beneficial ownership are treated separately unless supported by documentary proof.<\/p>\n\n\n\nKey Findings of the Report<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe report identifies a number of risk-relevant findings:<\/p>\n\n\n\n1. Dream Finance O\u00dc is the documented operating entity behind CoinsPaid and CryptoProcessing.<\/strong> Dream Finance O\u00dc\u2019s audited 2024 accounts<\/a> identify CoinsPaid<\/strong> (website<\/a>) and CryptoProcessing<\/strong> as its B2C and B2B brands and describe its business as crypto-payment processing, wallets, crypto\/fiat services, SaaS licensing, cross-chain recovery and OTC services.<\/p>\n\n\n\n2. The audited 2024 financials show significant scale and risk concentration.<\/strong> Dream Finance O\u00dc reported approximately \u20ac32.37 million in revenue<\/strong><\/a>, approximately \u20ac23.1 million in profit<\/strong>, and a major equity swing from negative to positive. The report also identifies a significant revenue concentration in Cura\u00e7ao<\/strong>, substantial customer-asset exposure, hacking-related losses, and a MiCA-related commercial impact.<\/p>\n\n\n\n3. Cura\u00e7ao exposure is a key compliance signal.<\/strong> The audited accounts show that a large part of 2024 revenue originated from Cura\u00e7ao. The accounts do not state that this revenue is casino revenue. However, Cura\u00e7ao is a major offshore iGaming jurisdiction, and this revenue concentration becomes compliance-relevant when considered together with the public iGaming positioning of CoinsPaid, CryptoProcessing and SoftSwiss-linked infrastructure.<\/p>\n\n\n\n4. MiCA and stablecoin restrictions had operational consequences.<\/strong> The report notes that the Dream Finance \/ CoinsPaid \/ CryptoProcessing structure faced MiCA-related transition issues, including stablecoin restrictions and contract terminations linked to stricter internal risk management.<\/p>\n\n\n\n5. Ownership and control remain a core unresolved issue.<\/strong> Public records identify Alexander Horst Riedinger<\/strong> as the registered 100% shareholder and UBO of Dream Finance O\u00dc. At the same time, insider accounts, litigation materials and third-party reporting have raised allegations of a different underlying control structure involving the SoftSwiss founder circle and other investors. The report does not resolve this dispute. Instead, it identifies the divergence between registered ownership and alleged economic control as a material compliance risk.<\/p>\n\n\n\n6. SoftSwiss, FinteqHub, CoinsPaid\/CryptoProcessing and Merkeleon are connected at founder, product and ecosystem level.<\/strong>Public founder-level material and entity self-positioning connect Ivan Montik, SoftSwiss, CoinsPaid\/CryptoProcessing and Merkeleon. SoftSwiss has also publicly described FinteqHub as a payment gateway built by its payments team. These links do not, by themselves, prove common equity ownership of all entities, but they justify close compliance scrutiny.<\/p>\n\n\n\n7. Payment-flow transparency remains incomplete.<\/strong> The report maps observed and alleged payment rails involving CoinsPaid, CryptoProcessing, FinteqHub, AlphaPo, open-banking providers, card\/acquiring layers, merchant descriptors and casino cashier patterns. Several payment chains are technically plausible and relevant, but remain unverified without PSP-side records, payment-page code, settlement instructions, contracts, or network captures.<\/p>\n\n\n\n8. The report records neutral and exculpatory facts.<\/strong> The report expressly notes that no MGA disciplinary action against Stable Aggregator was identified in the reviewed public record, that the group reports ISO 27001 certification, and that it states it complied with MiCA-related stablecoin requirements. These facts constrain any adverse conclusion and are included to preserve fairness.<\/p>\n\n\n\nWhat the Report Does Not Assert<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe report does not<\/strong> assert as established fact that money laundering, sanctions evasion, fraud, insolvency, or illegal payment processing has occurred.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt does not<\/strong> assert that any named individual is the legal beneficial owner of an entity contrary to public register data.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt does not<\/strong> assert that any licensed PSP, acquirer or bank knowingly processed payments for unlicensed merchants.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThese remain allegations, hypotheses, unresolved questions or risk indicators unless and until supported by stronger evidence or findings by competent authorities.<\/p>\n\n\n\nRecommendations for Regulators, Banks and PSPs<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe report recommends that regulators and supervisors review<\/strong> the beneficial-ownership question, the MiCA-related migration of activity, the role of FinteqHub as a possible payment-orchestration layer, and the relationship between platform provision, payment routing and downstream casino operators.<\/p>\n\n\n\nFor PSPs, acquirers and open-banking providers, the key question is merchant visibility<\/strong>: where a gateway or payment-orchestration layer sits between the regulated institution and the ultimate merchant, how clearly can the PSP identify, KYB-assess and monitor the real downstream operator?<\/p>\n\n\n\nFor banks and correspondent partners, the report recommends treating customer-asset balances, offshore revenue concentration, hacking history, crypto\/FX revaluation gains, and unresolved ownership questions as risk-relevant inputs to account and counterparty decisions.<\/p>\n\n\n\nRight of Reply<\/h2>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram again invites SoftSwiss<\/strong>, Stable Aggregator Limited<\/strong>, Dream Finance O\u00dc<\/strong>, Dream Finance UAB<\/strong>, Dream Transaction Lda<\/strong>, CoinsPaid<\/strong>, CryptoProcessing<\/strong>, FinteqHub<\/strong>, AlphaPo<\/strong>, and any relevant affiliated entity, officer or representative to provide a concise factual response.<\/p>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram remains prepared to review and publish factual, non-defamatory right-of-reply statements and to correct any demonstrably inaccurate factual assertion.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe report will remain a living compliance-intelligence dossier<\/strong> and may be updated as new documents, corrections, statements, regulatory developments or technical evidence become available.<\/p>\n\n\n\nCall for Information<\/h2>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram invites insiders, former employees, payment providers, compliance officers, regulators, law-enforcement officials, affected merchants, casino players, PSPs, acquirers, banks and other market participants to provide additional information.<\/p>\n\n\n\nWe are particularly interested in:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nprocessing contracts and settlement instructions involving SoftSwiss, FinteqHub, CoinsPaid, CryptoProcessing, AlphaPo or related casino operators;<\/li>\n\n\n\nmerchant-of-record records, cashier screenshots, payment descriptors and PSP routing data;<\/li>\n\n\n\ninternal KYB\/KYC, AML\/CFT, transaction-monitoring or SAR\/STR materials;<\/li>\n\n\n\ndocuments concerning Dream Finance O\u00dc, Dream Finance UAB, Dream Transaction Lda, FinteqHub, AlphaPo or related entities;<\/li>\n\n\n\nevidence concerning beneficial ownership, nominee structures, shareholder arrangements or control rights;<\/li>\n\n\n\ninformation about MiCA-related service suspensions, client migrations, stablecoin restrictions or de-risking measures;<\/li>\n\n\n\npayment-page code, network captures, wallet addresses, blockchain settlement data or transaction samples;<\/li>\n\n\n\nregulatory correspondence, enforcement notices or supervisory communications;<\/li>\n\n\n\ninformation concerning Cura\u00e7ao-linked casino operators, SoftSwiss-powered brands, payment orchestration or unlicensed-market exposure.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nInformation can be submitted securely via Whistle42.com<\/strong>.<\/p>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram protects sources and treats all submissions confidentially. Anonymous submissions are welcome, but documents, screenshots, contracts, emails, payment confirmations, blockchain data and other verifiable evidence are especially valuable.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nShare Strange Victory: Meloni, Hormuz, and the $300 Billion Price Tag of the Iran War"},"content":{"rendered":"\nFinancial Intelligence Commentary<\/strong><\/p>\n\n\n\nDonald Trump<\/strong>\u2019s \u201cvictory\u201d over Iran appears to have produced an unusual post-war ritual: the alleged loser gets oil waivers, sanctions relief, renewed access to global markets, a 60-day negotiation window, and a proposed reconstruction plan worth at least $300 billion. The alleged winner, meanwhile, is busy explaining the victory on Truth Social \u2014 and arguing with Italy\u2019s Giorgia Meloni<\/strong>.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt is, by any ordinary standard of power politics, a strange victory.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe latest episode began not in Tehran, Washington, or the Strait of Hormuz<\/a><\/strong>, but in a diplomatic quarrel<\/a> between Trump and Meloni after the G7 summit in France. Trump claimed that Meloni had repeatedly sought a photograph with him to improve her domestic popularity and that, after the US had \u201cdefeated Iran militarily,\u201d she now wanted to be friends again. Meloni rejected the claim sharply, telling Trump that her popularity was none of his concern and that being his friend had certainly not helped her in Italy.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat exchange matters because it exposes the deeper problem with Trump\u2019s Iran narrative. If this was such a clean American victory, why are allies distancing themselves, conservative voices questioning the deal, and the terms of the framework agreement looking less like surrender and more like a financially engineered exit ramp?<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe Victory Narrative Meets the Deal Sheet<\/h2>\n\n\n\nTrump presents the Iran framework as a hard-won military and diplomatic success. The deal itself tells a more complicated story.<\/p>\n\n\n\nAccording to the published summaries of the 14-point memorandum of understanding, the agreement provides for an immediate and permanent end to military operations, including in Lebanon. Iran may begin exporting oil and petroleum products once the memorandum is signed. The Strait of Hormuz is to be reopened for safe commercial passage for 60 days without charge. The parties are to resolve the disposition of Iran\u2019s stockpiled enriched material in further negotiations. Sanctions relief is linked to nuclear compliance and a final agreement, but temporary waivers for Iranian oil exports and associated services \u2014 including banking, insurance, and transport \u2014 are to be issued immediately.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is not the architecture of an unconditional surrender. It is the architecture of a negotiated pause.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe core nuclear questions are not finally resolved. The future of enriched material remains to be negotiated. Iran\u2019s broader strategic infrastructure \u2014 its missile capability, regional proxy relationships, and political system \u2014 remains intact. The framework appears to restore the negotiating table rather than impose defeat.<\/p>\n\n\n\nTrump may call it victory. A balance-sheet analyst might call it liability management.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe Meloni Moment<\/h2>\n\n\n\n<\/a><\/figure>\n<\/div>\n\n\nMeloni\u2019s rebuttal was politically significant<\/a> because she is not an obvious anti-Trump figure. She has long been viewed as one of Trump\u2019s closest ideological counterparts in Europe. Yet the Iran war placed her in an awkward position: close enough to Trump to be associated with the consequences, but not willing to subordinate Italian sovereignty to Washington\u2019s war strategy.<\/p>\n\n\n\nTrump criticized Italy<\/a> for not allowing the use of U.S. military bases in Italy during the Iran war. Meloni responded that the use of such bases is governed by agreements that Italy has always respected and that cannot simply be violated. \u201cAs long as I am prime minister, Italy remains a sovereign nation,\u201d she said in substance.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is the real Meloni moment. It is not about a photo. It is about the political cost of being seen as too close to Trump when the war\u2019s economic and strategic outcome is contested.<\/p>\n\n\n\nMeloni\u2019s message was clear: <\/strong>if Trump wants to sell the Iran framework as a glorious victory, he should not expect Italy to become part of the marketing campaign.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe Global Bill: Hormuz, Oil, Inflation<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe Iran war was never just a military event. It was a global financial shock.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe effective disruption of the Strait of Hormuz \u2014 one of the most important energy chokepoints in the world \u2014 created a historic oil and gas crisis. Before the war, the passage handled a major share of traded oil and natural gas. Its disruption pushed energy markets into crisis mode, drove oil and gas prices sharply higher, depleted emergency stockpiles, and fed directly into inflation expectations.<\/p>\n\n\n\nEven after the peace framework, the recovery is not automatic. Energy markets may welcome the reopening of Hormuz, but supply routes, insurance markets, shipping confidence, depleted inventories, and damaged infrastructure do not normalize by press release. A temporary reopening of the strait is relief, not resolution.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe economic mechanics are straightforward. Higher oil prices increase transport costs. Higher transport costs increase food, fertilizer, logistics, aviation, and industrial input costs. Inflation rises. Central banks hesitate. Growth slows. Consumers pay. Governments subsidize. Bond markets reprice risk.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIn other words: the war may have been fought in the Gulf, but the invoice was sent worldwide.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThis is where the \u201cvictory\u201d narrative becomes financially uncomfortable. If the war was won, it was won at the price of a global energy shock and a new inflationary impulse \u2014 precisely the kind of macroeconomic damage that political leaders usually try to avoid before elections.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe $300 Billion Question<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe most striking part of the framework agreement is the proposed reconstruction and economic development plan for Iran worth at least $300 billion.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe agreement does not formally describe this as \u201creparations.\u201d That legal distinction matters. But economically and politically, it has the look and feel of reparation-like reconstruction finance: a post-war funding architecture for the country Trump claims was defeated.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe administration has suggested that Gulf Arab states, not U.S. taxpayers, may provide much of the funding. That raises another question: why would Gulf states eagerly finance Iranian reconstruction after a war in which their own energy infrastructure and security model were exposed to severe risk?<\/p>\n\n\n\nEven if the United States does not directly write the check, Washington appears to be enabling the financial architecture. The deal opens pathways for Iranian oil exports, banking services, transport, insurance, potential asset access, and reconstruction capital. The defeated side is not being asked to pay. It is being offered mechanisms to recover.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is a curious form of victory.<\/p>\n\n\n\nOil Waivers: The Leverage Problem<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe timing of the oil waivers is particularly important. Under classic sanctions diplomacy, economic relief is the prize at the end of verified compliance. Under this framework, Iran receives immediate oil-export breathing space at the beginning of a 60-day negotiation period.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat reverses the leverage sequence.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIf Iran can sell oil more freely, access associated financial services, and wait for negotiations to unfold, Washington\u2019s pressure tool weakens before the most difficult concessions are secured. The enriched uranium question remains open. The broader military and regional questions remain open. The final agreement remains open.<\/p>\n\n\n\nTrump argues that the United States can resume military pressure if Iran fails to comply. That may be true in theory. But once oil flows restart, shipping normalizes, markets stabilize, and allies breathe a sigh of relief, the political cost of renewed escalation rises dramatically.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe framework therefore gives Trump an immediate domestic talking point \u2014 lower oil prices, reopening Hormuz, \u201cpeace\u201d \u2014 while giving Iran time, liquidity, and negotiating space.<\/p>\n\n\n\nEven Trump\u2019s Allies Are Not Convinced<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe criticism is not limited to Democrats or foreign-policy liberals. Conservative and Republican voices have also attacked the deal, arguing that Iran receives huge financial benefits without immediate dismantlement of its nuclear infrastructure, missile program, enriched uranium stockpile, or proxy networks.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat internal backlash is telling. When critics inside Trump\u2019s own political ecosystem describe the framework as weakness, appeasement, or even \u201cAmerican surrender,\u201d the word \u201cvictory\u201d becomes less an analytical conclusion than a branding exercise.<\/p>\n\n\n\nTrump\u2019s response has been characteristically combative: he has attacked the critics as fools, jealous, stupid, or bad people. But insulting critics does not resolve the balance sheet.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIf a victory needs this much explanation, perhaps it is not a victory. Perhaps it is a ceasefire packaged as triumph.<\/p>\n\n\n\nConclusion: The War Won on Truth Social<\/h2>\n\n\n\nTrump may have won the war on Truth Social. He may even win the domestic narrative if oil prices fall and voters accept the image of a president who bombed, negotiated, and declared victory.<\/p>\n\n\n\nBut the actual terms of the peace framework tell a less cinematic story.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIran survives. Iran negotiates. Iran exports oil. Iran may receive access to enormous reconstruction capital. The nuclear question is deferred. Hormuz remains a strategic lever. Allies are uneasy. Conservative critics are restless. Meloni is not volunteering for the victory parade.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThat is not necessarily defeat for America. But it is certainly not the clean triumph Trump claims.<\/p>\n\n\n\nIt is a strange victory \u2014 expensive, conditional, heavily financed, and suspiciously generous to the side that was supposedly beaten.<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nShare Information via Whistle42<\/a><\/div>\n<\/div>\n","protected":false},"excerpt":{"rendered":"Donald Trump calls the Iran framework a military victory. API dependencies, and direct catalogue-asset links around the Spinsopotamia anchor.<\/strong><\/h4>\n\n\n\nFinTelegram has publishing a new\u00a0Technical Annex<\/a><\/strong>\u00a0as a companion annex to its recently released\u00a0\u201cZentoria \/ Spinsopotamia and the NALMI Casino Network\u201d Compliance Intelligence Report<\/strong>. The new dossier does not replace the main report; it deepens the public-source technical case around the\u00a0Spinsopotamia.com<\/a><\/strong>\u00a0anchor with preserved HTML, exact telemetry and configuration markers, cross-domain API dependencies, and direct catalogue-level asset links that were not fully developed in the broader June report.<\/p>\n\n\n\nThe central conclusion remains unchanged. The evidence supports a strong technical and operational relationship between the Zentoria-facing\u00a0Spinsopotamia<\/strong>\u00a0anchor and a wider casino infrastructure concentrated inside the Marshall Islands-based\u00a0NALMI \/ AS213846<\/a> \u2013 185.207.196.0\/22<\/strong>\u00a0environment, while stopping short of claiming that\u00a0Zentoria Limited<\/strong>\u00a0legally owns every correlated domain or that a single ultimate beneficial owner controls the entire ecosystem.<\/p>\n\n\n\nKey findings<\/h2>\n\n\n\n\n83-domain strict configuration cluster:<\/strong>\u00a0Across 166 preserved result pages, 83 public domains co-exposed the same exact CSPER reporting account, the same exact SEON DNS token, the same non-financial seasonal-promotion response hash, and the same NALMI \/22 network envelope.<\/a><\/li>\n\n\n\nPreserved anchor evidence:<\/strong>\u00a0Two preserved URLScan result pages show Spinsopotamia with exact public identifiers, including a CSP reporting endpoint, SEON token, GTM container, GA4 measurement ID, Hotjar site identifier, CookieScript resource, and repeated platform-host family markers.<\/a><\/li>\n\n\n\nAPI dependency graph:<\/strong>\u00a0The dossier records 67 additional API hostnames and 98 direct cross-domain dependency pairs, supporting a rotating facade \/ canonical-host model rather than isolated standalone domains.<\/a><\/li>\n\n\n\nDirect catalogue linkage:<\/strong>\u00a0Six byte-identical public asset groups connect Spinsopotamia with comparison families including Kingmaker, BillyBets, 100Neon, BigClash, DuoSpin, LunuBet, and MyEmpire.<\/a><\/li>\n\n\n\nBroader NALMI continuity:<\/strong>\u00a0495 of 496 investigated domains remain inside the same\u00a0185.207.196.0\/22<\/strong>\u00a0routed envelope attributed to\u00a0AS213846 \/ NALMI LIMITED<\/strong>.<\/li>\n\n\n\nLegal-safe boundary preserved:<\/strong>\u00a0The dossier strengthens technical correlation, but it still does not establish common legal ownership, customer-account identity, or UBO attribution without provider-side records.<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nWhat the Technical Annex Adds!<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe strongest new contribution is the strict 83-domain shared-configuration cluster<\/strong>. Across 166 preserved result pages<\/strong>, the same exact CSPER reporting account<\/strong>, the same exact SEON DNS token<\/strong>, the same non-financial seasonal-promotion response hash, and the same compact NALMI network environment recur together across 83 public domains. This is materially stronger than generic provider overlap because these are account- or project-style markers, not merely ordinary use of common cloud or CDN infrastructure.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\n\nDownload the Zentoria \/ NALMI Technical Annex here<\/a><\/div>\n<\/div>\n\n\n\nThe dossier also documents preserved Spinsopotamia HTML evidence<\/strong> from two distinct URLScan result pages, one from October 2025 and one from March 2026, showing endpoint continuity from 185.207.197.250<\/strong> to 185.207.197.216<\/strong>, both inside the same 185.207.196.0\/22<\/strong> routed envelope. At the same time, the public HTML captures exact telemetry and configuration identifiers, including the CSPER reporting endpoint, the SEON DNS token, a Google Tag Manager<\/strong> container, a Google Analytics<\/strong> measurement ID, a Hotjar<\/strong> site identifier, and repeated platform hosts such as pg-nmga.com<\/strong>, pgf-euy2bt.com<\/strong>, and pgwhois.com<\/strong>.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\nFacade and canonical hosts<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe new dossier goes beyond static asset comparison by modelling a visible cross-domain API structure<\/strong>. Across the strict cluster, the preserved evidence records 67 additional API hosts<\/strong>, 150 unique technical nodes<\/strong>, and 98 direct cross-domain dependency pairs<\/strong>, showing that many public-facing casino domains rely on alternate or canonical backend hosts rather than serving as isolated sites.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\nThis matters because it supports a rotating facade \/ canonical-host architecture<\/strong>. In practical terms, public brand domains can change while the deeper API and operational structure remains stable, which is precisely the kind of pattern that regulators, PSPs, banks, and acquirers should treat as a high-priority disclosure target.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\nDirect cross-brand asset links<\/h2>\n\n\n\nA further step forward is the dossier\u2019s exact cross-brand catalogue evidence<\/strong>. It identifies six byte-identical public asset groups that directly connect Spinsopotamia<\/strong> with brands and families including Kingmaker, BillyBets, 100Neon, BigClash, DuoSpin, LunuBet,<\/strong> and MyEmpire<\/strong>.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\nThese are not visual similarities or naming overlaps. They are exact SHA-256-level matches in publicly delivered language and game-related assets, which materially strengthens the case that Spinsopotamia is embedded in a shared operational and content-delivery environment rather than standing alone as a separate casino endpoint.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\nWhat remains unchanged<\/h2>\n\n\n\nThe broader model from the main report still stands. The NALMI environment contains 495 of 496<\/strong> investigated domains inside the same 185.207.196.0\/22<\/strong> routed allocation, while a separate application-build supercluster links 98 domains<\/strong> through 120 meaningful byte-identical first-party asset groups<\/strong> across 16 technical components<\/strong>.<\/p>\n\n\n\nJust as importantly, the new dossier explicitly corrects several overbroad interpretations. It confirms that the verified target population is 496 domains<\/strong>, not 994; that generic regional support hosts do not prove a common tenant; that no raw technical identifier in the evidence directly proves NovaForge<\/strong>, Quadcode<\/strong>, Payabl<\/strong>, or Zentoria<\/strong> as technical owner; and that the evidence should not be stretched into a claim that all 495 NALMI-hosted domains<\/strong> are controlled by Zentoria. That narrowing is not a weakness; it improves the evidentiary quality of the case.<\/a><\/p>\n\n\n\nDownload both reports<\/h2>\n\n\n\nTo help regulators, payment institutions, banks, legal counsel, and investigative journalists assess the full record, FinTelegram is making both documents available together:<\/p>\n\n\n\n\nMain report:<\/strong>\u00a0Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia and the NALMI Casino Network \u2013 Compliance Intelligence Report<\/em>.<\/a><\/a><\/li>\n\n\n\nTechnical annex:<\/strong>\u00a0Zentoria \/ Spinsopotamia External Technical Evidence Dossier<\/em>.<\/a>Download the main report here:<\/strong>[Insert main report download link]Download the technical evidence dossier here:<\/strong>[Insert annex download link]<\/li>\n<\/ul>\n\n\n\nRead together, the two reports provide a layered public-source record: the main report maps the broader NALMI casino-domain environment, while the new dossier deepens the anchor case around Spinsopotamia<\/strong> itself.<\/p>\n\n\n\nCall for whistleblowers<\/h2>\n\n\n\nPublic-source OSINT can establish technical correlation, but provider-side records remain decisive for legal attribution.
